\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E\tr(|\Upsilon_{k}|^{r}\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}(\sum\limits_{(l,\gamma)}(S_{2}(t)U)^{T}Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U(Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U)^{T}S_{2}(t)U))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\E\tr(|\Upsilon_{k}|^{r}\E_{Z_{(l,\gamma),1}}(\sum\limits_{(l,\gamma)}(S_{2}(t)U)^{T}Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U(Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U)^{T}S_{2}(t)U))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\lVert|\Upsilon_{k}|^{r}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-2}}\cdot\lVert\E_{Z_{(l,\gamma),1}}(\sum_{(l,\gamma)}(S_{2}(t)U)^{T}Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U(Z_{(l,\gamma),1}U)^{T}S_{2}(t)U)\rVert_{q}

because
\eta
and
Z_{(l,\gamma),1}
are independent with
\Upsilon_{k}
and
S_{2}(t)
.

By Lemma
7.11
, we still get

\displaystyle(\text{factor 1})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{-1}(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{(1/q)(1/r)}

We can repeat the same argument for (factor 2) and show that

\displaystyle(\text{factor 2})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{-1}(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=k/2+1}^{k-1}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{(1/q)(1/r)}

Combining the estimates for (factor 1) and (factor 2) together, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[pm]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k-1}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}

Now, we have shown that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{(\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k})\in L_{\infty}(S_{\infty}^{d})^{k}}\frac{F(\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k})}{(\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k-1}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}})\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}

The final result follows from Corollary
6.6
.

∎

8.
Leverage Score Sparsified Embeddings

In this section, we prove our subspace embedding guarantee for the LESS-IC distribution, Theorem
3.10
. The proof is similar to the OSNAP case, and is accomplished via the following results,

•

Theorem
3.10
establishes the subspace embedding guarantee from a bound on the trace moments of the embedding error analogous to Theorem
3.2
in the OSNAP case.

•

Lemma
8.3
shows that it is sufficient to bound the moments of
(S_{1}U)^{T}S_{2}U+(S_{2}U)^{T}S_{1}U
to control the trace moments of the embedding error, analogous to Lemma
7.2
. This is the decoupling step.

•

Lemma
8.4
establishes that the entries of the LESS-IC distribution are uncorrelated and have variance
p
, which means the Gaussian model that we interpolate with should have independent entries with variance
p
, just as in the case of OSNAP.

•

Lemma
8.5
bounds the trace moments of the embedding error via interpolation after decoupling, analogous to Lemma
7.3
.

•

Lemma
8.6
establishes the differential inequality for the derivative of the interpolant, analogous to Lemma
7.5
in the OSNAP and OSE-IE case.

•

Lemma
8.7
establishes the trace inequality required to obtain the differential inequality in Lemma
8.6
, analogous to Lemma
7.8
in the case of OSNAP.

•

The trace inequality in Lemma
8.7
in turn requires a bound for the row norm moments of
S(t)U
. This is provided by Lemma
8.9
, analogous to Lemma
6.2
.

Before we proceed, we state the formal definition of the LESS-IC distribution. The construction is similar to OSNAP, with some changes to reflect the different number and size of subcolumns and the different scaling for non-zero entries across columns.

Definition 8.1

(LESS-IC)
.

Given
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
leverage scores
z_{1},...,z_{n}
, and
0<p<1
, define

\displaystyle b_{j}

\displaystyle:=\max\Big\{\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\right\rfloor,1\Big\}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{j}:=\left\lceil\frac{m}{b_{j}}\right\rceil.

An
m\times n
random matrix
S
is called a
K
-wise independent unscaled leverage score sparsified embedding with independent columns (
K
-wise independent unscaled LESS-IC), and also
\Pi=(1/\sqrt{pm})S
is called a
K
-wise independent LESS-IC, corresponding to
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-approximate leverage scores
(z_{1},...,z_{n})
with parameter
p
if it is distributed as

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\xi_{(l,\gamma_{l})}e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma_{l})}}e_{l}^{\top}

where in this expression

•

the collections
\{\xi_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
and
\{\mu_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
are mutually independent;

•

\{\xi_{l,\gamma_{l}}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
is a collection of
K
-wise independent Rademacher random variables;

•

\{\mu_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
is a collection of
K
-wise independent random variables such that each
\mu_{(l,\gamma_{l})}
is uniformly distributed in
[b_{l}(\gamma_{l}-1)+1:\min\{b_{l}\gamma_{l},m\}]
;

•

\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}:=\sqrt{p(\min\{b_{l}\gamma_{l},m\}-b_{l}(\gamma_{l}-1))}
;

•

e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma)}}
and
e_{l}
represent basis vectors in
\R^{m}
and
\R^{n}
respectively.

In addition, if all the random variables in the collections
\{\xi_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma\in[s]}
and
\{\mu_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma\in[s]}
are fully independent, then
S
is called a fully independent unscaled LESS-IC and
\Pi
is called a fully independent LESS-IC.

See
3.10

Remark 8.2
.

We can obtain a subspace embedding guarantee for the LESS-IE distribution by following the proof of Theorem
9.2
suitably modified for the case of LESS. One can check that Theorem
9.3
holds even when
S
has the LESS-IE distribution with the same values of
\sigma
and
R
. Thus, a subspace embedding for the LESS-IE distribution holds under the same conditions as Theorem
3.10
with the additional requirement
pm\geq\frac{c\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta})}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof of the main statement using Theorem
8.5
is identical to the proof of Theorem
3.2
. As in the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we shall assume that the collection of random variables
\{\xi_{l,\gamma_{l}}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
and
\{\mu_{l,\gamma_{l}}\}_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}
(See Definition
8.1
) are fully independent in our calculations, and since the proof proceeds via looking at moments of order
O(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))
, the same calculations still hold when we have
\log
-wise independence.

In this case, we require
q
to satisfy

pm\geq\left(\max\left\{c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.2}\sqrt{e}(q)^{5/2}/\varepsilon,c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.3}(q)^{3}\right\}\right)^{1+\frac{1}{q-2}}

. Just as in the case of OSNAP, it is enough to ensure that
c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.1}\frac{d+\log(d/\varepsilon\delta)}{(\varepsilon/\sqrt{e})^{2}}\leq m
, and

\displaystyle pm\geq

\displaystyle C_{1}\max\left\{\frac{C_{2}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{2.5}}{\varepsilon},C_{3}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{3}\right\}

where the form of the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
and
C_{3}
are analogous to those in (
7.1
), and set
q=\lceil 2\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta})\rceil+2
.

However, the value of
p
satisfying
pm\geq C_{1}\max\left\{\frac{C_{2}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{2.5}}{\varepsilon^{1+4/d}},C_{3}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{3}\right\}
has to be smaller than
1
for our construction to be defined. So we must also have

\displaystyle m\geq

\displaystyle C_{1}\max\left\{\frac{C_{2}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{2.5}}{\varepsilon},C_{3}(\log(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\delta}))^{3}\right\}

It is enough to ensure,

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\max\{C_{1}C_{2},C_{1}C_{3}\}\left(\log(d/\delta\varepsilon)\right)^{3}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\max\{C_{1}C_{2},C_{1}C_{3}\}\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)^{3}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{3\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}\log(d/\delta)}{\varepsilon}+\frac{3\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(d/\delta)^{2}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\log(d/\delta)^{3}}{\varepsilon}\right)

Now, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{3\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}\log(d/\delta)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{3\log(d/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad\frac{3\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(d/\delta)^{2}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\log(d/\delta)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}

So it is enough to have, for some
C_{4}>0
,

m\geq C_{4}\frac{d+\log(d/\delta)^{2}+\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\log(d/\delta)^{3}}{\varepsilon}

We proceed to estimate the number of non-zero entries. With reference to Definition
8.1
, we have,

b_{j}=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\right\rfloor\geq\frac{1}{2\beta_{1}pz_{j}}

when
\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\geq 1
. Thus,

s_{j}=\left\lceil\frac{m}{b_{j}}\right\rceil\leq\lceil 2\beta_{1}pmz_{j}\rceil\leq\max\left\{1,4\beta_{1}pmz_{j}\right\}

When
\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\leq 1
,

s_{j}=\left\lceil\frac{m}{b_{j}}\right\rceil=m\leq m\beta_{1}pz_{j}

The total number of non-zero entries in
S
is,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\leq n+\sum_{j=1}^{n}4\beta_{1}pmz_{j}\leq n+4\beta_{1}\beta_{2}pmd

∎

Lemma 8.3

(Decoupling)
.

When
S
has the fully independent unscaled LESS-IC distribution,

\displaystyle\E[\tr(U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d})^{2q}]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1,j\neq j^{\prime}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]

Consequently,

\displaystyle\E[\tr(U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d})^{2q}]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{S,S^{\prime}}\left[\tr\left(2\left((SU)^{T}S^{\prime}U+(SU)^{T}S^{\prime}U\right)\right)^{2q}\right]

where
S^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
S
.

Proof.

Letting
s_{ij}
denote the entries of
S
,

\displaystyle U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}U^{T}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}e_{j}\right)\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}s_{ij^{\prime}}e_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)U\right)-pm\cdot I_{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}u_{j}\right)\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)-pm\cdot I_{d}

where
u_{j}^{T}
denotes the
j\textsuperscript{th}
row of
U
. Separating the cases where
j=j^{\prime}
and
j\neq j^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}-pm\cdot I_{d}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}=1\\
j\neq j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}\right)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}-pm\cdot I_{d}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}=1\\
j\neq j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}

By construction,
\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}=\sum_{\gamma_{j}=1}^{s_{j}}\alpha_{(j,\gamma_{j})}^{2}=pm
, so,

\displaystyle U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}=1\\
j\neq j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}

From this point on, the proof is exactly the same as Lemma
7.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.4

(Variance and Uncorrelatedness)
.

Let
p=p_{m,n}\in(0,1]
and
S=\{s_{ij}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}
be a
m\times n
random matrix distributed according to the fully independent unscaled LESS-IC distributions. Then,
\E(s_{ij})=0
and
\operatorname{Var}(s_{ij})=p
for all
i\in[m],j\in[n]
, and
\cov(s_{i_{1}j_{1}},s_{i_{2}j_{2}})=0
for any
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}\subset[m],\{j_{1},j_{2}\}\subset[n]
and
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\E(s_{ij})=

\displaystyle\alpha_{(j,\gamma_{j})}^{2}\E(\xi_{j,\gamma_{j}})\Pb(\mu_{(j,\gamma_{l})}=i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{(j,\gamma_{j})}^{2}\cdot 0\cdot\Pb(\mu_{(j,\gamma_{l})}=i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

because
\E(\xi_{j,\gamma_{j}})=0
.

\displaystyle\E(s_{ij}^{2})=

\displaystyle\alpha_{(j,\gamma_{j})}^{2}\E(\xi_{j,\gamma_{j}}^{2})\Pb(\mu_{(j,\gamma_{l})}=i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sqrt{p(\min\{b_{l}\gamma_{l},m\}-b_{l}(\gamma_{l}-1))})^{2}\cdot 1\cdot\frac{1}{(\min\{b_{l}\gamma_{l},m\}-b_{l}(\gamma_{l}-1))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p

For the covariances, we first observe that
\cov(s_{i_{1}j_{1}},s_{i_{2}j_{2}})=0
if
(i_{1},j_{1})
and
(i_{2},j_{2})
belong to two different subcolumns by independence. If
(i_{1},j_{1})
and
(i_{2},j_{2})
belong to the same subcolumn, we have

\displaystyle\cov(s_{i_{1}j_{1}},s_{i_{2}j_{2}})=\E(s_{i_{1}j_{2}}s_{i_{2}j_{1}})=\E(0)=0

because each subcolumn has one hot distribution which means at most one of
s_{i_{1}j_{1}}
and
s_{i_{2}j_{2}}
can be nonzero.

∎

Using the decoupling result, we bound the trace moments of the embedding error by interpolating between LESS and its Gaussian model exactly as in the proof of Lemma
7.3
in Section
7.3
.

Lemma 8.5

(Trace Moments of Embedding Error for LESS)
.

Let
S
be an
m\times n
matrix distributed according to the fully independent unscaled LESS-IC distribution with parameter
p
for some fixed matrix
U
satisfying
U^{T}U=I
with given
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-approximate leverage scores. Define
X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{pm}}SU
. Given
0<\varepsilon<1
, there exist constants
c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.1},c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.2},c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.3}
such that for
m\geq c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.1}\frac{d+q}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and
q\in\N
satisfying
2\leq q\leq m
and
pm\geq\left(\max\left\{\frac{c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.2}q^{5/2}}{\varepsilon},c_{\ref*{prop:momestdecoupless}.3}q^{3}\right\}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{q-2}}
,

\displaystyle\E[\tr(X^{T}X-I_{d})^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq\varepsilon

Proof.

The structure of the proof is the same as the proof of Theorem
7.3
and we only highlight changes in the specific values. By Lemma
8.6
, we have,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\E[\tr\Gamma(t)^{2q}]\leq

\displaystyle\max\limits_{4\leq k\leq 2q}(c_{\ref*{lem:diffineqless}}q)^{k}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E[f(S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t))])^{1-\frac{k-2}{2q-2}}

As in the proof of Theorem
7.3
, we divide the analysis into two cases - (i)
pd<q^{2}
and (ii)
pd\geq q^{2}
.

In the first case, following the steps in the proof of Theorem
7.3
, we get,

\displaystyle(\E[\tr\Gamma(S_{1},S_{2})^{2q}])^{\alpha}-(\E[\tr\Gamma(G_{1},G_{2})^{2q}])^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{5}q)^{4}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2q}{q-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{q-1}+((c_{5}q)^{2q-4}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2q^{2}-4q}{q-1}})^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{5}q)^{4}(q)^{\frac{2q}{q-1}}(pm)^{\frac{2}{q-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{q-1}+((c_{5}q)^{2q-4}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}q)^{\frac{2q^{2}-4q}{q-1}})^{\frac{1}{q-2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c_{5}q)^{4}q^{2}(q)^{\frac{2}{q-1}}(pm)^{\frac{2}{q-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{q-1}+((c_{5}q)^{2}q^{\frac{2q}{q-1}}(pm)^{\frac{2}{q-1}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{6}q^{5}(pm)^{\frac{2}{q-1}}

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle(\E[\tr\Gamma(S_{1},S_{2})^{2q}])^{\frac{1}{q-1}}-(\E[\tr\Gamma(G_{1},G_{2})^{2q}])^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\leq c_{6}q^{5}(pm)^{\frac{2}{q-1}}

which gives,

\displaystyle(\E[\tr\Gamma(S_{1},S_{2})^{2q}])^{\frac{1}{2q}}-(\E[\tr\Gamma(G_{1},G_{2})^{2q}])^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq(c_{6}q^{5})^{\frac{q-1}{2q}}\leq c_{7}q^{5/2}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}

so in this case, we need
pm\geq c_{8}(q)^{5/2}/\varepsilon
.

The case when
pd>q^{2}
gives the same lower bound on
pm
as the corresponding case in Theorem
7.3
.

∎

Here we obtain the differential inequality that arises during interpolation in the proof of Lemma
8.5
.

Lemma 8.6

(Differential Inequality for LESS)
.

Let
p>0
. Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be independent random matrices with the fully independent unscaled LESS-IC distribution with parameter
p
for some fixed matrix
U
satisfying
U^{T}U=I
with given
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-approximate leverage scores. Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be independent random matrices with i.i.d. Guassian entries each with variance
p
, and define the interpolated random matrices,

(8.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{1}(t)=\sqrt{t}S_{1}+\sqrt{1-t}G_{1}\\
S_{2}(t)=\sqrt{t}S_{2}+\sqrt{1-t}G_{2}\end{split}

Let
f(M_{1},M_{2})=\tr(((M_{1}U)^{T}(M_{2}U)+(M_{2}U)^{T}(M_{1}U))^{2q})
.
Then there exists
c_{\ref*{lem:diffineqless}}>0
such that, for any
q\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\E[f(S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t))]\leq

\displaystyle\max\limits_{4\leq k\leq 2q}(c_{\ref*{lem:diffineqless}}q)^{k}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E[f(S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t))])^{1-\frac{k-2}{2q-2}}

Proof.

Following the steps in the proof of Lemma
7.5
, we get,

(8.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dt}\E[f(S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t))]\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=4}^{2q}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{P}([k])}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\,\\
&\E\Bigg[\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}\partial_{Z_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1|\pi}}\cdots\partial_{Z_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k|\pi}}f_{1,S_{2}(t)}(S_{1}(t))\Bigg]\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=4}^{2q}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{P}([k])}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\,\\
&\E\Bigg[\sum_{(l,\gamma_{l})\in[n]\times[pm]}\partial_{Z_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1|\pi}}\cdots\partial_{Z_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k|\pi}}f_{2,S_{1}(t)}(S_{2}(t))\Bigg]\\
=:&T_{1}+T_{2}\end{split}

where
f_{1,S_{2}(t)}(S_{1}(t))
and
f_{2,S_{1}(t)}(S_{2}(t))
are as in the proof of Lemma
7.5
and
Z_{(l,\gamma_{l}),j|\pi}
are constructed from
Z_{(l,\gamma_{l})}
appearing in
S
written as the sum
\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}Z_{l,\gamma_{l}}
when
S
has the unscaled LESS-IC distribution. Once again we remind the reader about our assumption that the
Z_{(l,\gamma_{l})}
are independent, with the additional remark that since all quantities involved are moments of order at most
4q
, the same calculations hold even if the
Z_{(l,\gamma)}
are
4q
-wise independent.

In this case, we use Lemma
8.7
to estimate (
8.2
) as in the proof of Lemma
7.5
to get,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\E[\tr\Gamma(t)^{2q}]\leq

\displaystyle\max\limits_{4\leq k\leq 2q}(c_{\ref*{lem:diffineqless}}q)^{k}((pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E[f(S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t))])^{1-\frac{k-2}{2q-2}}

∎

As in the oblivious case, a trace inequality is the key step in the proof of Lemma
8.6
.

Lemma 8.7

(Trace Inequalities for LESS)
.

Let
S_{1}(t)
and
S_{2}(t)
be as in Lemma
8.6
. Let
\Gamma(t)=(S_{1}(t)U)^{T}(S_{2}(t)U)+(S_{2}(t)U)^{T}(S_{1}(t)U)
. Let
\mathcal{Z}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma)}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}\cup\{\mu_{(l,\gamma)}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}
be a family of mutually independent random variables be the family of mutually independent random variables generating an instance of
S_{1}
with the unscaled LESS-IC distribution corresponding to some
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-approximate leverage scores for
U
. Let
q\geq 2
and
3\leq k\leq 2q
. Let
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
be a family of (possibly dependent) random elements, where for each
\lambda\in[k]
, the random element

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}\cup\{\mu_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}

has the same distribution as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma)}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}\cup\{\mu_{(l,\gamma)}:l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}

Let
Z_{(l,\gamma)}=\xi_{(l,\gamma)}e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma)}}e_{l}^{T}
and
Z_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}=\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}}e_{l}^{T}
.
Let
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
be a family of
L_{\infty}(S_{\infty}^{d})
random matrices.
Assume further that the collection
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
is independent of
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
, and
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
. (In other words,
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
can possibly be dependent with
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
.)
For each
l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]
and
\lambda\in k
, we define random vectors
\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,1},\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,2}\in\R^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,1}=\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}\alpha_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}u_{l}^{T}\text{ and }\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,2}=e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U

where
e_{\mu_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}}
represents the
\mu_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}
th coordinate vector. Then, given
0\leq\rho_{1},...,\rho_{k}\leq+\infty
such that
\sum\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\rho_{\lambda}}=1-\frac{k}{2q}
,
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\in\sym(\{1,2\})
, there exists
c_{\ref*{lem:traceineqless}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{lem:traceineqless}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{\beta pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\rho_{\lambda}}}

Proof.

The structure of the proof is exactly the same as the proof of Lemma
7.8
, and only the specific expressions differ. We define the functional
F(\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k})
exactly as in the proof of Lemma
7.8
, and proceed to prove the claim for when
\rho_{1}=\cdots=\rho_{k-1}=\infty
and
\rho_{k}=\frac{q}{q-k}
.

Let
\eta=(\eta(1),\eta(2))
be a uniformly distibuted random variable in
\{(l,\gamma_{l})|l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}
and for all
\lambda\in[k]
, define random variables
\Theta_{1,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,1}
and
\Theta_{2,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,2}
. Then, for
\mathcal{S}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}s_{l}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\cdot\E[\tr\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{\tau_{2}(1),2}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{2}(2),2}\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{\tau_{k}(1),k}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{k}(2),k}\Upsilon_{k}]

Defining factor 1 exactly as in Lemma
7.8
, we have,

\displaystyle(\text{factor 1})^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}\rVert_{2q}^{2-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=2}^{k/2}\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(1),\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(2),\lambda}\rVert_{2q}^{2}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\lVert|\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}|\Upsilon_{k}||\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}\rVert_{r}

To proceed, we prove an analogue of Lemma
7.10
to bound
\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(1),\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(2),\lambda}\rVert_{q}=\lVert\Theta_{1,\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{2,\lambda}\rVert_{q}
.

Lemma 8.8

(Product of Random Rows for LESS)
.

Let
\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,1},\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,2}\in\R^{d}
be as in Lemma
8.7
. Let
\eta=(\eta(1),\eta(2))
be a uniformly distributed random variable in
\{(l,\gamma_{l})|l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]\}
such that
\eta
is independent with
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
,
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
. For all
\lambda\in[k]
, define random vectors
\Theta_{1,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,1}
and
\Theta_{2,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,2}
. Let
q\geq 2
. Then there exists a constant
c_{\ref*{theta1theta2less}}>0
such that,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{1,\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{2,\lambda}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{\ref*{theta1theta2less}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}}{\mathcal{S}^{\frac{1}{q}}}

Proof.

Note that for each fixed
1\leq\lambda\leq k/2
,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{1,\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{2,\lambda}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle=\lVert\xi_{\eta,\lambda}\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E[\tr\lvert\xi_{\eta,\lambda}\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rvert^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=\left(\E[\tr\lvert\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rvert^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E\left[\frac{1}{d}\lVert\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\lVert e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Conditioning on
\eta
, we take expectation of the second factor over
\mu_{\eta,\lambda}
and
S_{2}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{1,\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{2,\lambda}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{1}{d}\lVert\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\E_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda},S_{2}(t)}[\lVert e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}]\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Note that, conditioned on
\eta
,
\mu_{\eta,\lambda}
is uniformly distributed over a subset of
[m]
. Therefore, by Lemma
8.9
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{1,\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{2,\lambda}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{1}{d}\lVert\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\E_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda},S_{2}(t)}[\lVert e_{\mu_{\eta,\lambda}}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}]\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{1}{d}\lVert\alpha_{\eta}u_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert^{q}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Note that when
b_{l}\geq m
, we have
s_{l}=1
and
\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert\leq\sqrt{pm}
. Moreover,
b_{l}\geq m
only when
1/\beta_{1}pz_{l}\geq m
, which means we have
pm\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}/\beta_{1}z_{l}\leq 1
. This means,
\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert^{q}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{q}\leq(pm\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2})^{q/2}\leq pm\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}
.

When
b_{l}<m
, we have
\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert\lVert u_{l}\rVert\leq\sqrt{pb_{j}}\lVert u_{l}\rVert\leq\lVert u_{l}\rVert/\sqrt{\beta_{1}z_{j}}\leq 1
when
\lfloor 1/\beta_{1}pz_{j}\rfloor\geq 1
and
b_{j}=\lfloor 1/\beta_{1}pz_{j}\rfloor
because
pb_{j}\leq 1/\beta_{1}z_{j}\leq 1/\lVert u_{j}\rVert^{2}
.
When
b_{j}=1
, we still have
\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert\lVert u_{l}\rVert\leq\sqrt{pb_{j}}\lVert u_{l}\rVert\leq\sqrt{p}\lVert u_{l}\rVert\leq 1
since
p\leq 1
.

So, we have
\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert^{q}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{q}\leq\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert^{2}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}\leq pb_{l}s_{l}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}
(Since
\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\leq\sqrt{pb_{l}}
by definition). But
s_{l}=\lceil m/b_{l}\rceil\leq 2m/b_{l}
. So,
pb_{l}s_{l}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}\leq 2pm\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}
.

We can now continue to bound,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{\gamma_{l}=1}^{s_{l}}\lvert\alpha_{(l,\gamma_{l})}\rvert^{q}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}2pm\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\frac{2pmd}{\mathcal{S}d}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(2pm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

∎

Using the same calculations as Lemma
7.8
for the term

\displaystyle\lVert|\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}|\Upsilon_{k}||\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}\rVert_{r}

we get

\displaystyle(\text{factor 1})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{c_{\ref*{theta1theta2less}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}}{\mathcal{S}^{\frac{1}{2q}}}\right)^{k-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\lVert|\Upsilon_{k}|^{r}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-2}}\cdot\left(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q})\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\left(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2less}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\right)^{k-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}\cdot\left(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q})\right)^{\frac{1}{qr}}

By repeating the same argument for factor 2, we get,

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2less}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k-1}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}

∎

Lemma 8.9

(Row Norm for LESS-IC)
.

Let
S(t):=\sqrt{t}S+\sqrt{1-t}G
, where
S
has the fully independent unscaled LESS-IC distribution as in Lemma
8.6
and
G
is an
m\times n
matrix with i.i.d. Gausian entries with variance
p
. Let
\mu
be a random variable uniformly distributed in
\phi\neq I\subset[m]
and independent of
S
and
G
. Then, there exists
c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\E_{\mu,S(t)}[\lVert e_{\mu}^{T}S(t)U\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:lessrownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}

Proof.

By Hölder’s inequality, it suffices to prove these bounds for moments of the order of the smallest even integer bigger than
q
, so without loss of generality, we may assume that
q
is an even integer. Moreover,

\displaystyle\E_{\mu,S(t)}[\lVert e_{\mu}^{T}S(t)U\rVert^{q}]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lvert I\rvert}\sum_{i\in I}\E_{S(t)}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}S(t)U\rVert^{q}]

In what follows, we fix
i\in I
, and obtain a bound for this fixed
i
. We observe that,

\displaystyle\E_{S(t)}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}S(t)U\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=\E_{S(t)}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}(\sqrt{t}S+\sqrt{1-t}G)U\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{S(t)}[\left(\lVert e_{i}^{T}SU\rVert+\lVert e_{i}^{T}GU\rVert\right)^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{S}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}SU\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}+\E_{G}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}GU\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}

Since
\E_{G}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}GU\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}
is simply the
q\textsuperscript{th}
moment of a
d
-dimensional Gaussian random vector whose independent components have variance
p
,
\E_{G}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}GU\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq c_{1}\sqrt{\max\{pd,pq\}}
, and it is enough to prove the bound in the statement of the lemma for
\E_{S}[\lVert e_{i}^{T}SU\rVert^{q}]^{\frac{1}{q}}
.

Note that in the case when
S
has the unscaled LESS-IC distribution,
S_{1,1}
is non-zero when the one hot distribution on the column submatrix
S_{[1:\min\{b_{1},m\}]\times{1}}
has it’s non zero entry on the first row. The probability that this happens is
1/\min\{b_{1},m\}=p/\alpha_{1,1}^{2}
. To generalise this to the entry
S_{i,j}
for a given
(i,j)
, observe that for each
(i,j)
, we can associate a unique tuple
(j,r)
with
r=r(i,j)
such that the random variable
\mu_{j,r}
determines whether
S_{ij}
is non zero.
\mu_{j,r}
is uniformly distributed in an interval of size
\alpha^{2}_{j,r}/p
, so the probability that
S_{ij}
is non-zero is
p/\alpha^{2}_{j,r}
. Moreover, we assume the columns of
S
are independent and remark that the calculations hold even for
\log
-wise independence since
\lVert e_{i}^{T}S(t)U\rVert^{q}
is a polynomial of order
q=O(\log(d/\varepsilon\delta))
in the entries of the first row of
S
only sees products of
O(\log(d/\varepsilon\delta))
many random variables.

From the above discussion, we can write,

\displaystyle e_{i}^{T}SU

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\xi_{i,j}u_{j}^{T}

\displaystyle=:\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}

where,

•

\{\delta_{ij}\}_{j\in[n]}
are independent Bernoulli random variables which are non zero with probability
p/\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}
respectively.

•

\{\xi_{i,j}\}_{j\in[n]}
is a collection of independent Rademacher random variables.

•

\{u_{j}^{T}\}_{j\in[n]}
are rows of the matrix
U
.

We shall use a recursive relation to estimate
\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}
, and proceed to obtain this recursive inequality,

(8.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&=\E\left[\left(\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&=\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}\|u_{j}\|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}=1\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}^{n}\alpha_{j_{1},r(i,j_{1})}\alpha_{j_{2},r(i,j_{2})}\delta_{ij_{1}}\delta_{ij_{2}}\xi_{i,j_{1}}\xi_{i,j_{2}}u_{j_{1}}^{\top}u_{j_{2}}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\leq\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}+\E\left[\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}=1\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}^{n}\alpha_{j_{1},r(i,j_{1})}\alpha_{j_{2},r(i,j_{2})}\delta_{ij_{1}}\delta_{ij_{2}}\xi_{i,j_{1}}\xi_{i,j_{2}}u_{j_{1}}^{\top}u_{j_{2}}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\end{split}

Using decoupling (
[
38
, Theorem 6.1.1]
),

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}=1\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}^{n}\alpha_{j_{1},r(i,j_{1})}\alpha_{j_{2},r(i,j_{2})}\delta_{ij_{1}}\delta_{ij_{2}}\xi_{i,j_{1}}\xi_{i,j_{2}}u_{j_{1}}^{\top}u_{j_{2}}\right)^{q}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\left(4\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}\alpha_{j_{1},r(i,j_{1})}\alpha_{j_{2},r(i,j_{2})}\delta_{ij_{1}}\widetilde{\delta}_{ij_{2}}\xi_{i,j_{1}}\widetilde{\xi}_{i,j_{2}}u_{j_{1}}^{\top}u_{j_{2}}\right)^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}u_{j}\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]

where
\{\widetilde{\delta}_{ij},\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}
are independent copies of
\{{\delta}_{ij},{\xi}_{ij}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}
.

Conditioning on
\{\widetilde{\delta}_{ij},\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}
, we have

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}u_{j}\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E\left[\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}u_{j}\right\rangle\right)^{q}\Bigg|\{\widetilde{\delta}_{ij},\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}\widetilde{\xi}_{ij}u_{j}\right\|^{q}\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]\right]

for some fixed unit vector
v\in\R^{d}
. Plugging this estimate back into (
8.3
),

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle+\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}

Both
\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}
and
\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}
shall be computed using tail probabilities obtained via Chernoff bounds. To this end, we first look at
\E[\exp(4\lambda\langle\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\rangle)]
for a fixed
j
,

\displaystyle\E\left[\exp\left(4\lambda\left\langle\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{p}{\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}}\left(\cosh\left(4\lambda\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\left\langle u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\cosh\left(4\lambda\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\left\langle u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)-1}{16\lambda^{2}\alpha^{2}_{j,r(i,j)}\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}}\cdot 16\lambda^{2}\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}p

Since
\cosh
is an even function, we may assume that
\langle u_{j},v\rangle\geq 0
. Then,
4\lambda\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\left\langle u_{j},v\right\rangle\leq 4\lambda\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\lVert u_{j}\rVert\leq 4\lambda\sqrt{pb_{j}}\lVert u_{j}\rVert\leq 4\lambda
when
\lfloor 1/\beta_{1}pz_{j}\rfloor\geq 1
and
b_{j}=\lfloor 1/\beta_{1}pz_{j}\rfloor
because
pb_{j}\leq 1/\beta_{1}z_{j}\leq 1/\lVert u_{j}\rVert^{2}
.
When
b_{j}=1
, we still have
4\lambda\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\left\langle u_{j},v\right\rangle\leq 4\lambda\sqrt{p}\cdot 1\leq 4\lambda
.

Using the fact that
\frac{\cosh(x)-1}{x^{2}}
is increasing in
x
for
x\geq 0
, we get,

\displaystyle\E\left[\exp\left(4\lambda\left\langle\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{\cosh(4\lambda)-1}{16\lambda^{2}}\cdot 16\lambda^{2}\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}p

\displaystyle\leq 1+\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}pe^{4\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}pe^{4\lambda})

So,

\displaystyle\E\left[\exp\left(4\lambda\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle u_{j},v\rangle^{2}pe^{4\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(pe^{4\lambda})

This gives a Chernoff tail bound,

\displaystyle\Pb\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right\rvert\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left(\frac{t}{4}\left(1-\log\left(\frac{t}{4p}\right)\right)\right)

and a standard moment computation gives,
\E\left[\left(4\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\delta_{ij}\xi_{ij}u_{j},v\right\rangle\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C_{1}q
.

To deal with
\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}
, we use the following Rosenthal’s inequality from
[
32
, p.442]
.

Lemma 8.10

(Theorem 14.10 from
[
32
]
)
.

Let
Z=\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{i}
where
X_{1},...,X_{n}
are independent and nonnegative random variables. Then the exists a constant
8.10
such that, for all integers
q\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle(\E(Z^{q}))^{1/q}\leq 2\E(Z)+c_{\ref*{lem:rosenthal}}q(\E((\max\limits_{j=1,...,n}X_{j})^{q}))^{1/q}

We use this lemma with
X_{j}=\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}
. We first calculate that

\displaystyle\E(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(p/\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2})\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}=pd

Next, we calculate
(\E((\max\limits_{j=1,...,n}X_{j})^{q}))^{1/q}
. Since
\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}\leq\sqrt{pb_{j}}
and
\delta_{ij}\leq 1
, we have
X_{j}=\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\leq pb_{j}\|u_{j}\|^{2}
. Now we consider two cases depending on whether
b_{j}=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\right\rfloor
or
b_{j}=1
. First, if
b_{j}=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\right\rfloor
, then we have
X_{j}\leq pb_{j}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\leq p\frac{1}{\beta_{1}pz_{j}}\beta_{1}z_{j}\leq 1
. Second, if
b_{j}=1
, then we have
X_{j}\leq pb_{j}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\leq p\leq 1
. In conclusion, we have
\max\limits_{j=1,...,n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\leq 1
almost surely, and therefore we have
(\E((\max\limits_{j=1,...,n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2})^{q}))^{1/q}\leq 1
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{j,r(i,j)}^{2}\|u_{j}\|^{2}\right)^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq 2pd+c_{2}q

for some constant
c_{2}
.

We thus have,

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2pd}+\sqrt{c_{2}q}+\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\sqrt{C_{1}q}

Let
C_{2}=\max\{2,c_{2},(3C_{1})^{2}\}
. Observe that
\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sqrt{pd}
. Assuming that

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{q}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq 3\sqrt{C_{2}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}

for some
q
, the above relation gives,

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z_{ij}\right\|^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{C_{2}pd}+\sqrt{C_{2}q}+\left(3\sqrt{C_{2}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{C_{1}q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\sqrt{C_{2}}+\sqrt{3C_{1}}C_{2}^{1/4}\right)\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sqrt{C_{2}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q^{2}\}}

∎

9.
Oblivious Subspace Embedding with Independent Entries

In this section, we consider the subspace embedding property for the following classical model with independent entries in the matrix
S
.

Definition 9.1

(OSE-IE)
.

An
m\times n
random matrix
S
is called an unscaled oblivious subspace embedding with independent entries (unscaled OSE-IE) with parameter
p
if
S
has i.i.d. entries
s_{i,j}=\delta_{(i,j)}\xi_{(i,j)}
where
\delta_{(i,j)}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables taking value 1 with probability
p\in(0,1]
and
\xi_{(i,j)}
are i.i.d. random variables independent with
\delta_{(i,j)}
and satisfy
\Pb(\xi_{(i,j)}=1)=\Pb(\xi_{(i,j)}=-1)=1/2
. And in this case,
\Pi=(1/\sqrt{pm})S
is call an OSE-IE with parameter
p
.

For this model, we have the following subspace embedding guarantee,

9.2
High Probability Bounds for the Embedding Error for OSE-IE.

Let
S
be an
m\times n
matrix distributed according to the unscaled OSE-IE distribution with parameter
p
. Let
U
be an arbitrary
n\times d
deterministic matrix such that
U^{T}U=I
. Then, there exist constants
c_{\ref*{thm:oseiemain}.1}>0
and
c_{\ref*{thm:oseiemain}.2}>0
such that for any
0<\varepsilon,\delta<1
and
d>10
,
we have

(9.1)

\displaystyle\Pb\left(1-\varepsilon\leq s_{\min}((1/\sqrt{pm})SU)\leq s_{\max}((1/\sqrt{pm})SU)\leq 1+\varepsilon\right)\geq 1-\delta

when
m>c_{\ref*{thm:oseiemain}.1}\frac{d+\log(1/\varepsilon\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and

pm\geq\min\left\{c_{\ref*{thm:oseiemain}.2}\left(\frac{(\log(d/\varepsilon\delta))^{2}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{(\log(d/\varepsilon\delta))}{\varepsilon^{2}}+(\log(d/\varepsilon\delta))^{3}\right),m\right\}

The overall structure of the proof of Theorem
9.2
is the same as the proof in the OSNAP case and we only highlight the differences from the proof of the OSNAP case discussed in Section
7
. A key difference between the above result and the corresponding result for OSNAP is the
1/\varepsilon^{2}
dependence in the lower bound for sparsity. This arises due to our approach of decomposing
U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d}
as

U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}\right)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}-pm\cdot I_{d}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}=1\\
j\neq j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}

where we label the former term as the diagonal term and the latter as the off diagonal term. In the OSNAP model, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}=pm
by construction and therefore the diagonal term vanishes, but when
S
has the OSE-IE distribution, we need not necessarily have
\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}=pm
, which means we need to analyze diagonal term in addition to the off-diagonal term analyzed in Lemma
7.2
. Observing that,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{ij}^{2}\right)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}-pm\cdot I_{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(s_{ij}^{2}-p\right)\right)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}

and using Minkowski’s inequality,

\displaystyle\E[\tr(U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d})^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1,j\neq j^{\prime}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

Proposition
9.3
controls the diagonal term (the former term), which gives rise to the
1/\varepsilon^{2}
dependence, and is analyzed in Section
9.1
. The analysis of the off-diagonal term (the latter term) is similar to the OSNAP case and is discussed in Section
9.2
.

9.1.
Controlling the diagonal term when
S
is the OSE-IE distribution

Proposition 9.3

(Diagonal Term)
.

Let
S
be an
m\times n
matrix distributed according to the unscaled OSE-IE distribution with parameter
p
. Let
U
be an arbitrary
n\times d
deterministic matrix such that
U^{T}U=I
. Given
0<\varepsilon<1
and
q\geq\log(d)
, there exist constants
c_{\ref*{prop:momestdiag}}
such that for
m\geq d\geq 20
and
pm\geq c_{\ref*{prop:momestdiag}}\frac{q}{\varepsilon^{2}}
,

\E\left[\tr\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq\varepsilon

Proposition
9.3
shows that, to make the diagonal term small, it suffices to require the nonzero entries per column to be greater than or equal to
c\frac{\log(d)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. Note that

\E\left[\tr\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\geq\E\left[\tr\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

and the latter can be of the order
1/\sqrt{pm}
when
U
corresponds to a
d
-dimensional coordinate subspace of
\R^{n}
. Thus, the
1/\varepsilon^{2}
dependence in the lower bound on
pm
is necessary when using this approach to prove the subspace embedding guarantee.

Proof.

First, note that,

\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq\E\left[\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right\rVert^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

and we shall use Matrix Bernstein’s inequality to estimate this moment. We shall only use a specific case of this theorem that holds more generally.

Lemma 9.4

(Matrix Bernstein, Theorem 6.1.1
[
12
]
)
.

For a finite sequence of
d\times d
independent mean zero symmetric bounded random matrices
\{S_{k}\}
with
\lVert S_{k}\rVert\leq R
, let,

\sigma=\left\lVert\sum_{k}\E[S^{2}_{k}]\right\rVert

Then,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\sum_{k}S_{k}\right\rVert\geq t\right)\leq 2d\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}/2}{\sigma+\frac{Rt}{3}}\right)

Recall that we are assuming that the entries of
S
are fully independent, but since we are only dealing with moments of order up to
4q
, the same bounds will hold even if the entries of
S
are
4q
-wise independent.

In our case, for the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}
, we have
R=1
and,

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\E[(s_{ij}^{2}-p)^{2}]\lVert u_{j}\rVert^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{n}pm(1-p)\lVert u_{j}\rVert^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq pm

where we use the fact that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1-p)\lVert u_{j}\rVert^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\prec\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}=I_{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right\rVert\geq t^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\exp\left(\frac{-(t^{\prime})^{2}/2}{pm+\frac{t}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)-I_{d}\right\rVert\geq\frac{t^{\prime}}{pm}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\exp\left(\frac{-(t^{\prime})^{2}/2}{pm+\frac{t}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)-I_{d}\right\rVert\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\exp\left(\frac{-(pmt)^{2}/2}{pm+\frac{pmt}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=2d\exp\left(\frac{-pmt^{2}/2}{1+\frac{t}{3}}\right)

Let
X
denote
\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{pm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{ij}^{2}u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)-I_{d}\right\rVert
for convenience. Then we have the mixed tail,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)\leq\begin{cases}2d\exp\left(-pmt^{2}/4\right)\text{ for }0\leq t\leq 3\\
2d\exp\left(-3pmt/4\right)\text{ for }3<t\\
\end{cases}

We are interested in
\E[X^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}
, and, for
\varepsilon<1
,

\displaystyle\E[X^{2q}]

\displaystyle=2q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle=2q\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt+2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt+2q\int_{3}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle\leq 2q\cdot\varepsilon^{2q}+2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt+2q\int_{3}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

When
pm\geq 4q/\varepsilon^{2}
,

\displaystyle 2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle\leq 2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}2d\exp\left(-pmt^{2}/4\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq 2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}2d\exp\left(-2qt^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}\right)dt

Using change of variables
\hat{t}=\sqrt{2q}t/\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle 2q\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle\leq 2q\cdot 2d\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2q}}\right)^{2q}\hat{t}^{2q-1}\exp\left(-\hat{t}^{2}/2\right)d\hat{t}

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2q}}\right)^{2q}\int_{0}^{\infty}\hat{t}^{2q-1}\exp\left(-\hat{t}^{2}/2\right)d\hat{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\pi}4qd\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2q-1}}{\sqrt{2q}}\right)^{2q}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\pi}4qd\left(\varepsilon\right)^{2q}

Continuing,

\displaystyle 2q\int_{3}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\int_{3}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\exp\left(-3pmt/4\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\int_{\varepsilon}^{3}t^{2q-1}\exp\left(-3qt/\varepsilon^{2}\right)dt

with the change of variables
\tilde{t}=3qt/\varepsilon^{2}
,

\displaystyle 2q\int_{3}^{\infty}t^{2q-1}\mathbb{P}(X\geq t)dt

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3q}\right)^{2q}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\tilde{t})^{2q-1}\exp\left(-\tilde{t}\right)d\tilde{t}

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}(2q-1)}{3q}\right)^{2q}

\displaystyle\leq 4qd\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)^{2q}

So,

\E[X^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq(18qd)^{\frac{1}{2q}}\varepsilon\leq\exp\left(\frac{2\log d+\log 18}{2q}\right)\varepsilon\leq e^{1.5}\varepsilon

By starting with
\varepsilon/e^{1.5}
instead, we can get the claim made in the statement.
∎

9.2.
Proving the subspace embedding guarantee for the OSE-IE distribution

In this section, we prove
9.2
.

See
9.2

Proof.

By the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we see that it is enough to show that
\E[\tr(X^{T}X-I_{d})^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq\varepsilon
, or, equivalently,
\E[\tr(U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d})^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq pm\varepsilon
whenever
p
and
q
satisfy
C\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\varepsilon}+q^{3}\right)\leq pm
and
m\geq C_{1}\frac{d+q}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
At the beginning of Section
9
, we saw that when
S
has the OSE-IE distribution,

\displaystyle\E[\tr(U^{T}S^{T}SU-pm\cdot I_{d})^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(s_{ij}^{2}-p)u_{j}u_{j}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1,j\neq j^{\prime}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

By Proposition
9.3
, the former term is bounded by
pm\varepsilon/2
when
pm\geq 4c_{\ref*{prop:momestdiag}}\frac{q}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and
m\geq 20
. For the latter term, we see that the proof of Lemma
7.2
still applies and we have,

\E\left[\tr\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1,j\neq j^{\prime}}^{n}s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}^{T}\right)^{2q}\right]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq 2\E[\tr(\Gamma(S_{1},S_{2}))^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

Following the proof of Theorem
7.3
and using Lemma
9.5
, we see that
\E[\tr(\Gamma(S_{1},S_{2}))^{2q}]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\leq\varepsilon/4
when
C\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\varepsilon}+q^{3}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{q-2}}\leq pm
and
m\geq C_{1}\frac{d+q}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. The claim follows as in the proof of Theorem
3.2
.
∎

9.3.
Trace Inequality in the OSE-IE Case

The trace inequality required to obtain the differential inequality for the interpolant between the moments of
(S_{1}U)^{T}S_{2}U
and
(G_{1}U)^{T}G_{2}U
has a slightly different proof in the OSE-IE case than in the OSNAP case, even though both bounds are the same.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
S_{1}(t)
and
S_{2}(t)
be as in Lemma
7.5
with both having the OSE-IE distribution. Let
\Gamma(t)=(S_{1}(t)U)^{T}(S_{2}(t)U)+(S_{2}(t)U)^{T}(S_{1}(t)U)
. Let
\mathcal{Z}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma)}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}\cup\{\delta_{(l,\gamma)}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}
be the family of mutually independent random variables generating an instance of
S_{1}
with the OSE-IE distribution. Let
q\geq 2
and
3\leq k\leq 2q
. Let
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
be a family of (possibly dependent) random elements, where for each
\lambda\in[k]
, the random element

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}\cup\{\delta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}

has the same distribution as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\{\xi_{(l,\gamma)}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}\cup\{\delta_{(l,\gamma)}:(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]\}

Let
Z_{(l,\gamma)}=\xi_{(l,\gamma)}\delta_{(l,\gamma)}e_{\gamma}e_{l}^{T}
and
Z_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}=\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}\delta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}e_{\gamma}e_{l}^{T}
.
Let
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
be a family of
L_{\infty}(S_{\infty}^{d})
random matrices.
Assume further that the collection
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
is independent of
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
, and
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
. (In other words,
\{\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k}\}
can possibly be dependent with
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
.)
For each
(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]
and
\lambda\in k
, we define random vectors
\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,1},\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,2}\in\R^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,1}=\xi_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}\delta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda}\mathbf{u}_{l}^{T}\text{ and }\Theta_{(l,\gamma),\lambda,2}=e_{\gamma}^{T}S_{2}(t)U

where
e_{\gamma}
represents the
\gamma
-th coordinate vector. Then, given
0\leq\beta_{1},...,\beta_{k}\leq+\infty
such that
\sum\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\beta_{\lambda}}=1-\frac{k}{2q}
,
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\in\sym(\{1,2\})
, there exists
c_{\ref*{lem:decouptraceineqose}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{(l,\gamma)\in[n]\times[m]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{lem:decouptraceineqose}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k}\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\beta_{\lambda}}

Proof.

The structure of the proof is exactly the same as the proof of Lemma
7.8
, and only the specific expressions differ. We define the functional
F(\Upsilon_{1},...,\Upsilon_{k})
exactly as in the proof of Lemma
7.8
, and proceed to prove the claim for when
\beta_{1}=\cdots=\beta_{k-1}=\infty
and
\beta_{k}=\frac{q}{q-k}
.

Let
\eta=(\eta(1),\eta(2))
be a uniformly distibuted random variable in
[n]\times[m]
and for all
\lambda\in[k]
, define random variables
\Theta_{1,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,1}
and
\Theta_{2,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,2}
. Then, for
\mathcal{S}=mn
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma\in[m]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\cdot\E[\tr\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{\tau_{2}(1),2}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{2}(2),2}\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{\tau_{k}(1),k}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{k}(2),k}\Upsilon_{k}]

Defining factor 1 exactly as in Lemma
7.8
, we have,

\displaystyle(\text{factor 1})^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}\rVert_{2q}^{2-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=2}^{k/2}\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(1),\lambda}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{\lambda}(2),\lambda}\rVert_{2q}^{2}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\lVert|\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}|\Upsilon_{k}||\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}\rVert_{r}

To proceed, we prove an analogue of Lemma
7.10
to bound
\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}
,

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j},\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\in\R^{d}
be as in Lemma
9.5
. Let
\eta=(\eta(1),\eta(2))
be a uniformly distributed random variable in
[n]\times[m]
such that
\eta
is independent with
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[k]}
,
S_{1},S_{2},G_{1},G_{2}
. For all
\lambda\in[k]
, define random vectors
\Theta_{1,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,1}
and
\Theta_{2,\lambda}=\Theta_{\eta,\lambda,2}
. Let
q\geq\log(pm)
. Then there exists a constant
c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}>0
such that,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}}{\mathcal{S}^{\frac{1}{q}}}

Proof.

Note that for each fixed
1\leq j\leq k/2
,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle=\lVert\xi_{\eta}\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E[\tr\lvert\xi_{\eta}\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rvert^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=\left(\E[\tr\lvert\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rvert^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E\left[\frac{1}{d}\lVert\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\lVert e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Conditioning on
\eta
, we take expectation of the first factor over
\delta_{\eta}
and the second factor over
S_{2}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{1}{d}\E_{\delta_{\eta}}\left[\lVert\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\right]\E_{S_{2}(t)}[\lVert e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}]\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Note that, after conditioning on
\eta
,
\eta(2)
is fixed. In other words,
\eta(2)
is uniformly distributed over a one element subset of
[m]
. Therefore, by Lemma
6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\Theta_{\tau_{j}(1),j}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{j}(2),j}\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{1}{d}\E_{\delta_{\eta}}\left[\lVert\delta_{\eta}\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\right]\E_{S_{2}(t)}[\lVert e_{\eta(2)}^{T}S_{2}(t)U\rVert^{q}]\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:rownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}\left(\E_{\eta}\left[\frac{p}{d}\lVert\mathbf{u}_{\eta(1)}\rVert^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:rownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}\left(\frac{p}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{m}\lVert\mathbf{u}_{l}\rVert^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=c_{\ref*{lem:rownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}\left(\frac{pm}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ref*{lem:rownormbound}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}\left(\frac{pm}{\mathcal{S}d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}(pm)^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}}{\mathcal{S}^{\frac{1}{q}}}

where in the last line we use the fact that
\sum_{l=1}^{n}\lVert u_{l}\rVert^{2}=d
.

∎

Using the same calculations as Lemma
7.8
for the term

\displaystyle\lVert|\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}|\Upsilon_{k}||\Theta_{\tau_{1}(2),1}^{T}\Theta_{\tau_{1}(1),1}|^{1/r}\rVert_{r}

we get

\displaystyle(\text{factor 1})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}}{\mathcal{S}^{\frac{1}{2q}}}\right)^{k-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\lVert|\Upsilon_{k}|^{r}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-2}}\cdot\left(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q})\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}}\left(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}}\right)^{k-2/r}\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k/2}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}\cdot\left(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q})\right)^{\frac{1}{qr}}

By repeating the same argument for factor 2, we get,

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n],\gamma_{l}\in[s_{l}]}\E[\tr\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),1,\tau_{1}(2)}\Upsilon_{1}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),2,\tau_{2}(2)}

\displaystyle\cdots\Upsilon_{2}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(1)}^{T}\Theta_{(l,\gamma_{l}),k,\tau_{k}(2)}\Upsilon_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c_{\ref*{theta1theta2ose}}\sqrt{\max\{pd,q\}})^{\frac{2qk-4q}{2q-2}}(\E\tr((\Gamma(t))^{2q}))^{\frac{1}{q}\cdot\frac{2q-k}{2q-2}}({\prod\limits_{\lambda=1}^{k-1}{\lVert\Upsilon_{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}})\cdot\lVert\Upsilon_{k}\rVert_{\frac{2q}{2q-k}}}

∎

10.
Conclusions

We give an oblivious subspace embedding with optimal embedding dimension that achieves near-optimal sparsity, thus nearly matching a conjecture of Nelson and Nguyen in terms of the best sparsity attainable by an optimal oblivious subspace embedding. We also propose a fast algorithm for constructing low-distortion subspace embeddings, based on a new family of Leverage Score Sparsified embeddings with Independent Columns (LESS-IC). This new algorithm leads to speedups in downstream applications such as optimization problems based on constrained or regularized least squares. As a by-product of our analysis, we develop a new set of tools for matrix universality, combining a decoupling argument with a two-dimensional interpolation method, which are likely of independent interest.
